Let me understand the task. We need to prove strong normalization for STLC with CBV semantics, locally nameless.

The theorem statement is `normalization : forall t T, <{ empty |-- t \in T }> -> strongly_normalizing t`.

Note: The typing judgment is for terms with free variables too (context). But the theorem only asserts closed terms (empty context). Nice.

However, closed terms in locally nameless — the theorem says any term typed in empty context. But a term can be `tm_fvar x` typed in empty? No, empty x = None, so T_Var can't apply with empty context. So all typed terms are locally closed? Actually not necessarily: `tm_bvar` is never typable. Free variables can't be typed with empty. Since typing rules only introduce free variables via T_Abs opening with fvar x under context x|->T1; in empty context that would need empty x = Some T1 which is false. So terms typed in empty are locally closed, presumably. But we need to prove it or maybe not — for SN we might not need local closure necessarily? Actually the step relation requires local closure in some rules, but SN doesn't require it directly.

Plan: standard Girard/Tait logical relation for CBV STLC with strong normalization.

Approach: Since this is call-by-value, the standard logical relation:

- For base type Bool: SN(t) ∧ value(t)? Actually for CBV, "hereditarily terminating" terms: define
  - SN_terms at Bool: t is SN.
  - At arrow T1 -> T2: t is SN ∧ (∀ v, value v → T1-rel v → T2-rel (t v)). Hmm, but for the fundamental theorem, we need the relation applied to arbitrary terms (not just values), because application of terms.

The standard "hereditary termination" for CBV: HT_T(t) := SN(t) ∧ (if T = Bool, then value t implies t = true or t = false; if T = A->B, then for all u, HT_A(u) implies HT_B(t u)). Actually the "value" clause is needed for adequacy (closed terms of Bool reduce to values). For SN theorem alone, we may not need the value clause. But to prove fundamental theorem for T_Abs, we need abstraction applied to arguments reduces. Let's think.

Let's define:
- SN t := strongly_normalizing t (given inductively, equivalent to Acc step t? need to show equivalence or use well-founded induction).

Key issue: strongly_normalizing is an inductive proposition. We need to do induction on SN derivations. We can prove equivalence with Acc step t:

Lemma SN_iff_Acc: forall t, strongly_normalizing t <-> Acc step t.

Then use well-founded induction on Acc for the logical relation proofs.

Alternatively, define logical relation directly in terms of strongly_normalizing and prove things by induction on SN derivations.

Let me think about the standard approach, used in e.g. Software Foundations "StlcProp" / "Norm" chapter (Pierce's TAPL, chapter 12 "Normalization"). Actually SF has a chapter "Norm" proving normalization for STLC with CBV using a logical relation with SN. Let me recall.

Hmm, no — for CBV, application `t1 t2` where t2 is a term satisfying relation. The fundamental theorem then proves for closed terms.

Actually there's a subtlety: in CBV, `t1 t2` steps require t1 to be a value to apply ST_App2, etc. Strong normalization of `t1 t2` given sn t1 and sn t2 requires reasoning about the app case: if t1 not a value, it steps; if t1 is a value and t2 not value, t2 steps; if both values and t1 = abs, beta. So the relation needs something about values.

Standard approach (from TAPL chapter 12 "Normalization"): They prove that if t is in the logical relation R_T then sn t, and the fundamental lemma: Gamma |- t : T implies t in R_T under any well-formed substitution. Actually TAPL uses a different proof for CBV: they prove normalization for CBV by defining "t is normalizing" and showing preservation under evaluation contexts.

But actually the cleanest known approach for locally nameless + CBV + SN: use a logical relation defined on "closed" terms with respect to a closing substitution, or use the "hereditary substitution" / semantic typing.

Alternative simpler approach: Prove SN via a "reduction in the term" approach: SN is preserved by application etc. Actually, there's a well-known direct approach: define an "evaluation" relation and prove strong normalization by structural induction using a relation on terms with substitution.

Hmm, that seems off. Let me look at the standard SF definition:

```
Fixpoint open_rec (k : nat) (u : tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => if beq_nat k i then u else (tm_bvar i)
  | tm_fvar x => tm_fvar x
```

```
Fixpoint open_rec (k : nat) (u : tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => if k =? i then u else tm_bvar i
```

and the convention: To open the outermost abstraction `\ T t1`, you call `open t1 u = open_rec 0 u t1` (on the body t1 directly, not on the abs term!). Yes! That's it. So everything is consistent. In this convention, de Bruijn indices are "absolute depth from the term root", not relative. Fine. This is the "locally nameless with indices counting from outside" convention used in SF.

Given that, substitution-ish lemmas: We'll want properties of open for SN proofs. Let me now design the proof.

Goal: forall t T, empty |-- t : T -> SN t.

Standard logical relation approach (Girard's reducibility candidates / Tait's method) adapted to CBV.

Define the candidate / semantic typing relation. Since terms can have free variables (fvar), we interpret via closing substitution: a term t is semantically of type T under context Gamma if for every closing substitution sigma mapping each x : Gamma x = Some S to a value of semantic type S, sigma(t) is in the relation for T.

Alternatively, since the theorem is only about empty context, we could do: define relation R_T on closed-by-substitution terms.

But we need to handle the T_Abs case: Gamma |-- λ:T1.t1 : T1→T2 means for all x not in L, update Gamma x T1 |-- open t1 (fvar x) : T2. By IH, the opened body is semantically of T2 under extended context. Need to show λ is semantically of T1→T2: for any value v of type T1 (closed in the extended sense), (λ.t1) v --> open t1 v is in R_T2. Semantically, we need a lemma: if for all x, open t1 (fvar x) is in R_T2 under Gamma,x:T1, then for any v in R_T1 (value), open t1 v is in R_T2. That's the standard "beta lemma" requiring the semantic property to be "closed under replacing fvar x by arbitrary related term". We can prove this by induction on T2 (the "rename/open" lemma), or use a substitution lemma parameterized.

Alternative approach avoiding substitutions: define the logical relation on all terms (including open ones) as a relation indexed by an environment of semantic values for free variables, then show:
- R_T is closed under "fvar substitution": if t ∈ R_T[rho] and v ∈ V_S[rho] then t[x:=v] ∈ R_T[rho]... where rho maps variables. But the environment handles it.

Hmm, we can simplify: define semantic typing as:

`sem_ty (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop`, for terms that may contain fvars, parameterized... Actually simplest: define for each type T a predicate on terms with no free bvars (locally closed) *and* with an environment mapping each fvar to a value... This is getting complex but that's how it's done.

Alternative approach: avoid the environment entirely by defining the relation on *closed* terms (in the sense of locally_closed and no fvars, i.e., "absolutely closed"). Then fundamental theorem for empty context only: prove forall t T, empty |-- t : T -> sem T t. But T_Abs introduces free variable x: `x |-> T1 ; empty |-- open t1 (fvar x) : T2`. We'd need to handle the context. So we do need context/substitution interpretation.

OK here's a cleaner strategy that's often used and robust in Coq: Use the "semantic typing with closing substitutions" where the substitution is a function `atom -> tm` mapping each fvar to a *value*, and define:

- `value_sem T v`: semantic value relation (only for values).
- `term_sem T t rho` (rho : atom -> tm is a closing substitution of semantic values): t under substitution rho is in the term relation.

Actually we can combine: define `R T t` = term relation for *closed* terms only (closed = no fvars, no bvars; but need a predicate for that). Then define the interpretation of a context: rho such that for each x, rho x is a closed value in R_{Gamma(x)}. And prove: for Gamma |-- t : T and rho closing Gamma, R T (apply rho t). Hmm, applying substitution requires substitution machinery. This is doable but more code.

Alternative approach avoiding substitution application: Extend the logical relation to terms with free variables via the environment. That's essentially the same work: we need to prove that R is closed under fvar-substitution, i.e., if `R T t (rho)` and `V S v (rho)` then `R T (t[x:=v]) rho`. Hmm.

Alternatively: Prove strong normalization directly without a logical relation on terms with free variables: First prove a "context weakening/strengthening" and use the fact that the theorem only concerns closed terms. We could prove the fundamental theorem in the form:

for all Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> for all sigma (a substitution from atoms to closed SN values?), ... 

Hmm.

Actually here's a cleaner way that avoids substitution entirely: define the relation as a predicate on *terms considered relative to an environment of fvar-to-term assignments*. Define:

`Fixpoint R (T : ty) (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : Prop := ...`

where R is "t is SN in the environment rho" — but t may contain fvars; we need to evaluate fvars through rho. We can define a "closing" operation `close rho t` via recursion substituting fvars with rho's terms, and define `R T rho t := R T (close rho t)` for closed relation. But then we need SN of close rho t.

Alternatively, keep track: define `sem T t` where t is any term, and simultaneously a relation `env Gamma rho` on substitutions, then prove:

Lemma fundamental: forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall rho, env Gamma rho -> sem T (subst rho t).

And we need substitution composition lemmas. This is all standard but somewhat lengthy.

Another thought: maybe there's a much more direct approach specific to this system. Let's think: the language has Bool, arrow, if. CBV. We want strong normalization of closed well-typed terms. Could we prove it by a direct combinatorial argument?

One classic direct approach for STLC SN (for the full beta, non-CBV): Tait's method needs the logical relation. For CBV, we additionally need to handle "stuck" terms (non-values). 

Alternative direct approach: Prove by induction on the typing derivation, using a strengthened induction hypothesis about well-typed terms and their applications. Hmm.

Alternative: Prove SN via a well-founded measure: assign to each term a "size"/complexity such that each step decreases it. For STLC, we can use the standard "type-erasure to simply typed lambda calculus and each CBV step corresponds to a nonempty sequence of beta steps + administrative steps"? Hmm, CBV steps can be simulated by beta, but the if-steps are just administrative. Measure approach: assign each term a natural number (e.g., size of the term's normal form under full beta? no...).

Actually, we could reduce CBV strong normalization to a simpler notion: Define an "evaluation" semantics that computes values by recursion on the term structure (for well-typed terms), and show each step decreases a measure. But showing the measure decreases requires knowing normal forms.

Hmm, the logical relation is the standard, and I know how to do it. Let me plan carefully with the locally nameless setup.

Honestly, I'll design my own proof. Let me set up the locally nameless machinery carefully.

### Design

Definedness: We want to prove: for all t T, has_type empty t T -> SN t, where SN = strongly_normalizing (the given inductive).

I'll prove everything in terms of `Acc step t` and then convert.

```
Lemma SN_Acc : forall t, strongly_normalizing t <-> Acc step t.
```
Actually `strongly_normalizing t` is defined as the inductive with constructor SN_intro t (forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'). This is exactly `Acc step t` up to definitional equality? `Acc` is:

```
Inductive Acc (R : X -> X -> Prop) (x : X) : Prop :=
  Acc_intro : (forall y, R x y -> Acc R y) -> Acc R x.
```

So `strongly_normalizing t` and `Acc step t` are *definitionally* the same structure but different inductives. We can prove iff by induction trivially. Note that well-founded induction on `step` via Acc: define `SN_ind'`: forall P, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) -> forall t, SN t -> P t. That's just induction on the SN derivation, which is easy: `induction 2` or something. Actually `strongly_normalizing` is already an accessibility-like inductive, so we can do induction on it directly! Since it's defined precisely as the accessible predicate, the induction principle is exactly the well-founded induction principle:

```
strongly_normalizing_ind :
 forall P : tm -> Prop,
 (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
 forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t
```

Wait, check: the induction principle generated for `strongly_normalizing`:

```
strongly_normalizing_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop,
  (forall t : tm, (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
  forall t : tm, strongly_normalizing t -> P t
```

Hmm, because the constructor has a recursive argument (forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'). So induction gives P only after showing for all t' step t t' -> P t', but the IH is nested under the dependent argument. Coq generates the principle with the "forall t' step t t' -> P t'" as an assumption. Let me recall: for `Acc`, the generated induction principle is exactly well-founded induction:

```
Acc_ind : forall (R : X -> X -> Prop) (P : X -> Prop),
```

```
  match t with
  | tm_fvar y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else tm_fvar y
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst x u t1) (subst x u t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst x u t1)
  | ...
  end.
```

**Cleaner route: use the "hereditary termination" / evaluation-based definition or a different candidate definition that is easier to work with for if.**

Standard Tait-style for CBV in TAPL Chapter 12: They define R_T for *closed* terms as follows:

Big-step based definitions handle `if` naturally because eval of if requires eval of the guard.

Actually here's a cleaner idea specific to our language: **The if-term's SN and R can be reduced to the SN/R of its components via the fact that the if construct is "congruence-like"**. Note that R at arrow type is: SN ∧ ∀u. R A u → R B (t u). Define instead:

R T t := SN t ∧ (forall u, R A u → R B (t u)) — with the arrow clause requiring R B (t u) for all u. For the if-term s = if b t2 t3, we want R T s. By induction on T:
- T = Bool: SN ✓.
- T = A→B: Need forall u ∈ R A, R B (s u). Since R B (s u) is the relation at the smaller type B, we have the IH for B available! If we're doing induction on T, then R_if for type A→B needs R_if-ish for type B applied to... hmm, s u is not an if-term.

OK here's another thought. What if we define the logical relation as **hereditarily SN under all "applications with SN-arguments"**? I.e., define R in a "neutral-friendly" way:

R T t := SN t ∧ (match T with
  | Bool => True
  | A→B => forall u, R A u -> R B (t u)
  end)

and prove the key "expansion" property (if t is neutral, SN t, and all reducts in R T, then R T t) as a *mutual induction with the definition*, i.e., prove it by induction on T and then well-founded induction on SN t. Let's carefully attempt:

**Lemma R_expand (by induction on T):** for all t, lc0 t → ~ value t → SN t → (forall t', t --> t' → R T t') → R T t.

Case T = Bool: R Bool t = SN t ∧ lc0 t ✓.

Case T = A→B: Need lc0, SN ✓ (given), and ∀u, R A u → R B (t u). So fix u with R A u; need R B (t u). 
Apply IH_B (R_expand at B) to the term (t u) with:
- lc0 (t u) ✓
- ~ value (t u) ✓ (app is not a value)
- SN (t u): need to prove.
- forall w, t u --> w → R B w.

For the last: if t u --> w, the step is:
  * ST_AppAbs: t = abs → contradicts ~value t.
  * ST_App1: t --> t', w = t' u. Then R T t' gives the arrow clause: R B (t' u) ✓ (t' ∈ R (A→B) since t --> t', by hypothesis).
  * ST_App2: value t → contradicts ~value t.
So all reducts of t u are in R B ✓.

Now SN (t u): by SN_intro, need all reducts of t u SN. The reducts are t' u as above, and R B (t' u) → SN (t' u) ✓ (property R_implies_SN at B, which is part of what we prove by induction on T... but here B is a smaller type, so if we're proving properties by induction on T, we have R_implies_SN at B available. But careful: R_implies_SN at B needs the arrow clause's u to be... no, R B w → SN w is proved by induction on B, with the arrow case using SN explicitly in the definition. Actually in this definition, R T t includes SN t as a conjunct at every type, so R B w → SN w is immediate by projection! ✓✓.

But wait: to show SN (t u), I need to invoke SN_intro on all reducts, and that requires the reducts to be SN, which follows from R B (t' u) — but I need SN of t' u for the *SN_intro at t u*, which is fine since R B (t' u) → SN (t' u) by projection ✓. But hold on: SN_intro requires a proof of (forall w, t u --> w → strongly_normalizing w). We established each reduct is in R B, and R B → SN. ✓.

Then R_expand at B applies: lc0 ✓, ~value ✓, SN ✓, all reducts in R B ✓ → R B (t u). ✓

Oh nice, this works! The key point: SN (t u) came from the reducts being in R B (which includes SN), and the reducts are in R B because t's reducts are in R (A→B). And crucially, since ~value t, no beta at the top of t u.

But wait, there's a subtlety: the term `t u` in the expansion lemma's conclusion — for the arrow clause, we're proving R (A→B) t. We needed SN (t u). We showed all reducts of t u are in R B, hence SN. ✓.

And we need ~value t (given), lc0 t (given), all reducts of t in R (A→B) (given). ✓.

Now, where do we use R_expand? For the if-term: s = if b t2 t3. We need R T s. Is s neutral (~value)? ✓ (if is not abs/true/false). lc0 ✓. SN s: need to prove from SN b, SN t2, SN t3 + R facts. All reducts of s: 
- if b = true: t2. R T t2 ✓ given. SN t2 ✓.
- if b = false: t3 ✓.
- if b --> b': if b' t2 t3. Need R T (if b' t2 t3) — by well-founded induction on SN b! So prove by induction on SN b: for all (or with fixed t2,t3), if R T t2, R T t3, lc0 t2, lc0 t3, then SN (if b t2 t3) and all reducts in R T.

Hmm, R_expand needs "all reducts in R T" AND "SN s". We can prove both simultaneously by well-founded induction on SN b:

For fixed t2 t3 with R T t2, R T t3 (and lc0 for the step rules), prove: for all b with R Bool b (= SN b ∧ lc0 b), SN (if b t2 t3) ∧ (forall s', if b t2 t3 --> s' -> R T s').

Induction on SN b:
- Reducts: 
  * ST_IfTrue: b = true, s' = t2, R T t2 ✓; SN t2 ✓.
  * ST_IfFalse: ✓.
  * ST_If: b --> b', s' = if b' t2 t3; need R T (if b' t2 t3).   Wait, R_expand requires the term to be neutral and SN with all reducts in R T. We have exactly that for if b' t2 t3. So R T (if b' t2 t3) ✓.

Hmm wait, there's a circularity concern: R_expand at type T is proved by induction on T, and its proof at arrow type uses R_expand at B (smaller) and R_implies_SN at B — fine. But in the if-proof at type T = A→B, we use R_expand at T. That's fine — R_expand is already proven (by induction on the type) before we use it.

But in R_expand's proof at arrow type, we needed `R B (t' u)` where t' ∈ R (A→B) — the arrow clause of R (A→B) at t' with argument u — fine. And SN (t u) from all reducts in R B — fine. And R_expand at B applied to t u. All good. This is a well-founded recursion on the type structure plus SN structure, and the SN structure is only used in the sense of SN_intro: to prove SN (t u), we need all its reducts to be SN, which they are because they're in R B (smaller type). No induction on SN proof needed for that — we directly construct SN using SN_intro.

**Lemma R_abs:** Given lc0 (abs T1 t1) [equivalently lc_at 1 t1] and (forall x fresh, R T2 (open t1 (fvar x))), show R (T1→T2) (abs T1 t1).

OK so let's combine: keep the environment-based fundamental theorem (subst_all rho, env_sem), where R itself is the simple SN-based relation above. Now, do we actually need env_sem and subst_all, or can we simplify the fundamental theorem to avoid them? Consider proving:

**Fundamental theorem attempt 2:** forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall rho, (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (rho x)) -> R T (subst_all rho t).

We need subst_all and its lemmas:
- subst_all rho (tm_app t1 t2) = tm_app (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2) (by definition ✓)
- subst_all rho (tm_abs T t1) = tm_abs T (subst_all rho t1) (by definition ✓)
- subst_all rho (tm_if ...) similar ✓
- subst_all rho (tm_fvar x) = rho x — actually if we define subst_all with `if x =? y then rho x else ...` weird; simpler: subst_all rho (tm_fvar x) := rho x. Since substitution is total on free vars, just replace fvar x by rho x.
- subst_all rho (tm_bvar i) = tm_bvar i ✓.

Then the opening lemma needed: subst x u (open t1 (fvar x)) = open t1 u for x ∉ FV(t1). But if rho is "identity except x:=u", then subst_all rho (open t1 (fvar x)) = subst x u (open t1 (fvar x)) — where rho y = fvar y for y ≠ x. So define `subst_all` such that it coincides. Fine.

We need FV and freshness lemmas:
- FV : tm -> list atom? Or a predicate `occurs x t`. Let's define:
```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom := ...
```
Hmm, but T_Abs gives us a list L of atoms and the hypothesis forall x, ~In x L -> ... We need to pick x not in L and not in FV(t1) (so that the open/subst lemma applies). We need a lemma: for any two lists L1 L2, exists x, ~ In x L1 /\ ~ In x L2. With atoms = nat, easy: take x = max of both lists + 1, or use a simple construction. Alternatively define FV as a predicate and derive a list. Let's just define:

```
Fixpoint fv_list (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fv_list t1) (fv_list t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv_list t1
  | tm_true | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => app (fv_list t1) (app (fv_list t2) (fv_list t3))
  end.
```

Now, the well-founded induction on SN b: we use `strongly_normalizing` as the accessibility predicate. Hmm, actually the induction hypothesis for the premise (which is itself a forall-quantified family) — when we do induction on the typing derivation, the T_Abs case has a premise `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...`. The induction hypothesis will be `forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` where P is the statement we're proving (forall rho, env_sem ... -> R T2 (...)). Yes, if we state the theorem as:

Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall rho, env_sem Gamma rho -> R T (subst_all rho t).

and do `induction 1` — hmm, the IH for the premise `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...` would be `forall x, ~ In x L -> forall rho, env_sem (update Gamma x T1) rho -> R T2 (subst_all rho (open t1 (fvar x)))`. Yes, when the premise is a universally quantified formula, Coq's induction gives an IH with the quantifier + IH on the has_type part. Let me double check: `induction H` where H : has_type Gamma t T. For T_Abs, the constructor is `T_Abs : forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) -> has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1 --> T2)`. The induction scheme generated includes as IH: `forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`. Yes, Coq handles nested foralls in premises fine (since the recursive occurrence is under binders, it generalizes).

Wait, but there's a catch: `induction 1` on a statement with parameters (rho) — we need to keep rho general or reintroduce it. Since the statement is `forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall rho, env_sem Gamma rho -> R T (subst_all rho t)`, we do `intros Gamma t T H. induction H; intros rho Henv.` Actually, better: `intros Gamma t T H; induction H; intros rho Henv` — rho and Henv get introduced after induction so the IH is general over rho ✓ (since they were introduced after H, they're not in the induction context... hmm, actually Coq's `induction H` reverts nothing; but if rho/Henv are not yet introduced, they won't be fixed). Let's write:

```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall rho, env_sem Gamma rho -> R T (subst_all rho t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H; intros rho Henv.
```
Hmm, the statement after `intros Gamma t T H` is `forall rho, env_sem Gamma rho -> R T (subst_all rho t)`. Then `induction H` — Coq will complain that the conclusion depends on... Actually `induction H` when the goal is a forall over rho works: Coq generalizes the goal with respect to the conclusion (it will produce goals `forall rho, env_sem ... -> R ...` and IHs similarly). This is standard. But the IH might not be general enough if the goal mentions Gamma/t/T which depend on H's indices. To be safe, use `remember` or `generalize dependent`. Standard approach:

```
induction H; intros rho Henv.
```
should work; Coq automatically reverts the goal's dependence on the indices (Gamma, t, T) — actually `induction H` where H : has_type Gamma t T and Gamma, t, T are arbitrary variables in context: Coq will revert Gamma t T (since H depends on them), do induction, then reintroduce. Hmm, `induction H` reverts the dependent hypotheses automatically? I believe `induction H` behaves like: it generalizes the goal over the indices of H and then does induction, giving IH with the same statement. Yes, in practice `induction H` on an inductive with indices in context works well when the goal is a proposition depending on those indices.

OK. Now let's spell out all the definitions and lemmas needed:

### Definitions

```coq
(* Free variables as list *)
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom := ...

(* Single substitution *)
Fixpoint subst (x : atom) (u t : tm) : tm := ...

(* Simultaneous substitution *)
Fixpoint subst_all (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...

(* Local closure helpers: not needed beyond lc_at given *)

(* R *)
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Wait, careful with Fixpoint on ty with a Prop-valued function whose recursive call is on T2 (structurally smaller) ✓. But the definition `R` has `forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (...)` — the recursive calls are on T1 and T2, both subterms of Ty_Arrow T1 T2 ✓. Coq accepts nested recursion in a Fixpoint returning Prop? Yes, `Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop := match T with ... end` should be fine since the recursive calls are on strict subterms. However, R T1 u for T1 and R T2 (app t u) — both on subterms ✓. Coq's guard checker should accept.

Hmm, but the "positivity" of this definition: it's a Fixpoint, not an Inductive, so no strict positivity requirement issues; the guard condition just needs structural recursion ✓.

Actually, one concern: `R T1 u` inside the definition where u is bound — fine.

### env_sem

```coq
Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (rho : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (rho x).
```

### Lemmas

1. **SN facts:**
   - `SN_step : forall t t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t -> strongly_normalizing t'` — by destruct on the SN. Actually `strongly_normalizing t` destructs to `forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'` ✓:
     ```
     Lemma SN_step : forall t t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t -> strongly_normalizing t'.
     Proof. intros t t' Hstep Hsn. destruct Hsn as [t H]. apply H. assumption. Qed.
     ```
     Hmm, destruct on Hsn : strongly_normalizing t gives H : forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'. Wait, the constructor SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> strongly_normalizing t. Destructing gives exactly the function ✓ (the t is refined to the actual one).

     ```

     ```

   - `SN_ind` / well-founded induction: `induction Hsn` directly.
   - `SN_app_value_abs`: maybe not needed separately.

2. **lc facts:**

4. **R facts:**
   - `R_lc : forall T t, R T t -> locally_closed t` — projection (by destruct on T).

   - `R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'` — induction on T:
     * Bool: lc0 t' (step_lc) ∧ SN t' (SN_step).

   Hmm wait, but actually, do we even need R_fvar? Other fvar y get (update rho x v) y = rho y (since y ≠ x; do we know y ≠ x? If y = x we'd be in the x case, so other occurrences have y ≠ x — but wait, "update rho x v y" where y = x reduces to v; for the RHS, we have subst_all rho t1 then open with v: pre-existing fvar x in t1 would become rho x — but there are none ✓. So for y ≠ x, update gives rho y ✓.) And bvars: in LHS, open_rec k inserts fvar x at bvar k positions, then substitution replaces with v. In RHS, open_rec k (subst_all rho t1) v inserts v at those positions... but careful: the bvars in t1 at position k — in LHS they're replaced by fvar x by open_rec, then by v by subst. In RHS, subst_all rho t1 keeps bvar k (substitution doesn't touch bvars), then open_rec k replaces with v. ✓ Same positions. And bvars inside rho y images: LHS leaves them alone (subst_all doesn't touch bvars of the *substituted terms*); RHS: open_rec k on (subst_all rho t1) may descend into rho y images and replace bvar k there! Hmm, wait: does open_rec descend into inserted terms? open_rec k (subst_all rho t1) v: subst_all rho t1 is a syntactic term that includes rho y's images inline; open_rec traverses the whole term, so it would replace bvar k occurrences *inside* the rho y images too. Whereas in LHS, subst_all (update rho x v) (open_rec k (fvar x) t1): open_rec k (fvar x) t1 traverses t1 only (not the images, which aren't inserted yet), then subst_all inserts rho y images without modification. So they differ if rho y contains bvar k!
   
   So we DO need the hypothesis that rho's images are locally closed (no bvars), which we have from env_sem (R S (rho y) → lc0 (rho y)) ✓. Great, so the lemma needs that hypothesis. Since env_sem gives it, we're fine.

   Let me restate the lemma:
   ```
   Lemma subst_all_open_rec : forall rho,
     (forall y, locally_closed (rho y)) ->
     forall x u t k, ~ In x (fv t) ->
       subst_all (update_env rho x u) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k (subst_all rho t) u.
   ```
   Hmm, but in the bvar case we need `open_rec k (tm_fvar x) (tm_bvar i)` computed; fine.

5. **The fundamental theorem.**

```
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall rho, env_sem Gamma rho -> R T (subst_all rho t).
```

Proof by induction on the typing derivation. Cases:

- T_Var: Gamma x = Some T. Goal: R T (subst_all rho (tm_fvar x)) = R T (rho x). Henv x T H ✓.
- T_Abs: Goal: R (T1→T2) (subst_all rho (tm_abs T1 t1)) — note subst_all rho (tm_abs T1 t1) = tm_abs T1 (subst_all rho t1) by definition.
  Unfold R. Need:
  (a) locally_closed (tm_abs T1 (subst_all rho t1)): i.e., lc_at 1 (subst_all rho t1).
      - From typing: lc0 t1 (prove has_type_lc separately) → lc_at 1 t1 (weaken).
      - rho images lc0 from env_sem: for each y, need R (Gamma y)?? Hmm! env_sem says for y with Gamma y = Some S, R S (rho y) → lc0 (rho y). But if Gamma y = None, rho y is unconstrained! So rho y could have bvars. Uh oh.
      
      Hmm. So we need env_sem to include lc0 for ALL y (not just those in the context). Let's strengthen env_sem:
      ```
      Definition env_sem Gamma rho :=
        (forall y, locally_closed (rho y)) /\ (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (rho x)).
      ```
      Then lc of rho images ✓ for the substitution lemma and lc preservation.
      
      Hmm, but then at the top level, env_sem empty rho requires forall y, lc0 (rho y) — choose rho := fun _ => tm_true ✓.
      
      And in the T_Abs case, env_sem (update Gamma x T1) (update_env rho x v) needs: forall y, lc0 (update_env rho x v y) — need lc0 v ✓ (from R T1 v → lc0 v) and lc0 (rho y) ✓.
      
      Good, strengthen env_sem.

  (b) strongly_normalizing (tm_abs T1 (subst_all rho t1)): SN_abs ✓.
  (c) forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app (tm_abs T1 (subst_all rho t1)) u).
      Apply R_abs_app with body := subst_all rho t1:
      - lc0 (tm_abs T1 (subst_all rho t1)) ✓ from (a).
      - beta hypothesis: forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (open (subst_all rho t1) v).
        Given v with R T1 v. Pick x fresh: x ∉ L, x ∉ fv t1. Get Hprem := H x Hx (the typing of open t1 (fvar x) under update Gamma x T1). Apply IH to Hprem with rho' := update_env rho x v:
          - env_sem (update Gamma x T1) rho': 
            * forall y, lc0 (rho' y): if y = x → lc0 v ✓; else lc0 (rho y) ✓.
            * forall y S, update Gamma x T1 y = Some S -> R S (rho' y): if y = x then S = T1 and rho' x = v, need R T1 v ✓ (careful: update Gamma x T1 x = Some T1 and the equation gives Some T1 = Some S → S = T1 ✓). If y ≠ x: update Gamma x T1 y = Gamma y, rho' y = rho y, use env_sem ✓.
          - Result: R T2 (subst_all (update_env rho x v) (open t1 (fvar x))).
          - Rewrite using subst_all_open_rec with x ∉ fv t1: = R T2 (open (subst_all rho t1) v) ✓.
      - R T1 u ✓ from the arrow clause hypothesis.
      Then R_abs_app gives R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst_all rho t1)) u) ✓.

- T_App: IH1, IH2. Goal: R T2 (subst_all rho (tm_app t1 t2)) = R T2 (tm_app (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2)). From IH1's arrow clause with u := subst_all rho t2 ✓.

- T_True / T_False: Goal: R Bool (subst_all rho tm_true) = R Bool tm_true. Need lc0 tm_true ✓, SN tm_true ✓ (no steps). ✓

- T_If: Goal: R T (tm_if (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2) (subst_all rho t3)). Apply R_if with b := subst_all rho t1 etc. Need R Bool b ✓ (IH1), R T t2' ✓ (IH2), R T t3' ✓ (IH3). ✓

And `has_type_lc : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> locally_closed t` — inductive proof:
- T_Var: lc0 (fvar x) ✓.
- T_Abs: need lc_at 0 (tm_abs T1 t1) = lc_at 1 t1. From IH on a *fresh* x: lc0 (open t1 (fvar x)) = lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1). Need lc_at 1 t1. 6. **Final theorem:**
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T, empty |-- t : T -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T H.
  pose proof (fundamental empty t T H rho_id hend) as HR.
  (* HR : R T (subst_all rho_id t) *)

  (* Need subst_all rho_id t = t *)
  ...
Qed.
```
Choose rho := fun x => tm_fvar x. Then subst_all rho t = t (need lemma: subst_all fvar t = t, by induction on t). And env_sem empty rho: (forall y, lc0 (fvar y)) ✓ and (forall x T, empty x = Some T -> R T (fvar x)) — vacuous since empty x = None ≠ Some T ✓.

So `normalization` follows from R_sn with subst_all fvar t = t.

### Remaining details

**Definition of `fv`:**

```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.
```

**Definition of subst:**

```
Fixpoint subst (x : atom) (u : tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else tm_fvar y
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst x u t1) (subst x u t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst x u t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst x u t1) (subst x u t2) (subst x u t3)
  end.
```

**Definition of subst_all:**

```
Fixpoint subst_all (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar y => rho y
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_all rho t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2) (subst_all rho t3)
  end.
```

**update_env:**
```
Definition update_env (rho : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else rho y.
```

**R_expand statement and proof details.**

```
Lemma R_expand : forall T t,
  locally_closed t ->
  ~ value t ->
  strongly_normalizing t ->
  (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') ->
  R T t.
Proof.
  induction T; intros t Hlc Hnv Hsn Hreds; simpl.
  - (* Bool *) auto.
  - (* Arrow T1 T2 *) split; [auto|]. split; [auto|].
    intros u Hu.
    apply IHT2.  (* R_expand at T2 *)
    + (* lc0 (t u) *) constructor; [apply R_lc with (T:=T1) in Hu|]; auto.
      (* locally_closed (app t u) = lc_at 0 (app t u) via lc_app *)
    + (* ~ value (t u) *) intros Hv; inversion Hv.
    + (* SN (t u) *) apply SN_intro; intros w Hw.
      destruct (step_app_inv ...) hmm.
```
For the last two, I need: SN (t u) and all reducts of (t u) in R T2. Both from case analysis on the step `t u --> w`:
  - ST_AppAbs: t = abs → contradicts ~value t (need value (abs) which follows from locally_closed (abs) given in the step derivation — inversion gives `locally_closed (tm_abs T t0)`, then v_abs gives value (tm_abs T t0) = value t, contradiction).
  - ST_App1: t --> t0, w = app t0 u. R T2 (app t0 u) from the arrow clause of Hreds t0 Hstep ✓ (R (T1→T2) t0 → arrow clause with u : Hu).
  - ST_App2: value t → contradiction.

So define a lemma:
```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall t u w,
  tm_app t u --> w ->
  (exists T0 body v, t = tm_abs T0 body /\ value u /\ w = open body v ...) 
```
Hmm, let me write a cleaner inversion lemma:

```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall t u w,
  tm_app t u --> w ->
  (exists T0 body, t = tm_abs T0 body /\ value u /\ w = open body u) \/
  (exists t', t --> t' /\ w = tm_app t' u) \/
  (exists u', value t /\ u --> u' /\ w = tm_app t u').
```
Proof by inversion on the step. In ST_AppAbs: t = abs T0 body, v = u, w = open body u → left ✓. ST_App1: t --> t', w = app t' u → middle ✓. ST_App2: value t, u --> u', w = app t u' → right ✓.

Then:
- For the step case, we need the actual t to not be an abstraction (from ~ value t) — in ST_AppAbs, we get `locally_closed (tm_abs T0 body)`, so `value (tm_abs T0 body)` by v_abs, and t = tm_abs T0 body, contradicting Hnv ✓.
- ST_App2 similarly: value t contradicts Hnv ✓.
- ST_App1: use Hreds.

Good. And for `SN (t u)`: apply SN_intro; intros w Hw; destruct (step_app_inv Hw): 
  - ST_AppAbs case: contradiction.
  - ST_App1: R T2 (app t' u) from arrow clause of Hreds t' Hstep; then R_sn ✓.

```
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.
```

```
Proof.
  intros T b t2 t3 Hb H2 H3.
  destruct Hb as [Hlc_b Hsn_b].
  assert (H : forall b, strongly_normalizing b -> locally_closed b ->
     R T t2 -> R T t3 -> strongly_normalizing (tm_if b t2 t3) /\

    - (* SN (if b t2 t3) *) apply SN_intro; intros s' Hs'.
      destruct (step_if_inv ...) ...
    - (* reducts in R T *)
      ...
```

where step_if_inv:
```
Lemma step_if_inv : forall b t2 t3 s,
  tm_if b t2 t3 --> s ->
  (b = tm_true /\ s = t2) \/ (b = tm_false /\ s = t3) \/ (exists b', b --> b' /\ s = tm_if b' t2 t3).
```
Proof by inversion ✓.

For SN (if b t2 t3): intros s' Hs'; destruct step_if_inv:
  - b = true, s' = t2: SN t2 from R_sn ✓ (subst).
  - b = false: SN t3 ✓.
  - b --> b', s' = if b' t2 t3: IH b' Hstep gives conjunction → SN ✓. Note IH : forall b', step b b' -> lc0 b' -> R T t2 -> R T t3 -> (SN (if b' t2 t3) ∧ ...). Apply with Hlcb' — need lc0 b'. From Hlcb (lc0 b) and step b b' → step_lc ✓.

For reducts in R T: destruct step_if_inv:
  - b = true, s' = t2: R T t2 ✓.
  - b = false, s' = t3 ✓.
  - b --> b', s' = if b' t2 t3: apply R_expand at T with t := if b' t2 t3:
    * lc0 (if b'...) from lc0 b' (step_lc) and R_lc t2 t3 ✓.
    * ~ value (if ...) ✓ (inversion on value).
    * SN ✓ from IH conjunction.
    * all reducts in R T ✓ from IH conjunction.

Then finally: R T (if b t2 t3) via R_expand:
  - lc0 ✓ (from Hlc_b, R_lc t2, R_lc t3)
  - ~ value ✓
  - SN ✓ from H conjunction
  - all reducts ✓ from H conjunction.
✓

Note: R_expand is proven before R_if ✓ (no circularity: R_expand is by induction on T; R_if uses R_expand at T and T is arbitrary).

Standard pattern:
```
assert (H : forall b, strongly_normalizing b -> locally_closed b -> R T t2 -> R T t3 -> ...).

```
Here the goal at `induction Hsn` is `lc0 b -> R T t2 -> R T t3 -> ...` with b fixed (as a variable in context) and Hsn : SN b. Coq's induction will fail if the goal mentions b in a way that's not generalized... Actually the standard behavior: `induction Hsn` where Hsn : SN b, b is a variable, and the goal mentions b — Coq automatically generalizes the goal over b? I believe `induction H` does "revert the hypotheses that depend on the indices" — Hmm. Let me recall: If you have `b : tm`, `Hsn : SN b`, goal `P b`, then `induction Hsn` works and gives `forall b', step b b' -> P b'` as IH, because the induction principle for `SN` is `SN_ind : forall P, (forall b, (forall b', step b b' -> SN b') -> (forall b', step b b' -> P b') -> P b) -> forall b, SN b -> P b`. To apply this, the goal `P b` must be generalized to `P`, and b is fixed. Coq handles this by abstracting over b in the goal. Yes, `induction Hsn` does that automatically (it's like `induction Hsn generalizing b`? no...). Hmm. Actually, when you do `induction Hsn`, Coq looks at the conclusion `P b` where b is the index; it will "revert" b if b is a variable in the local context. I'm fairly confident this works — it's the standard way to do well-founded induction with Acc. E.g., in Coq, `intros n H; induction H.` where H : Acc R n and goal P n works.

Hmm, but to be safe, I could state H with explicit quantification and use the induction principle directly:
```
intros T b0 t2 t3 Hb H2 H3.
destruct Hb as [Hlcb Hsnb].
pose proof (strongly_normalizing_ind (fun b => locally_closed b -> R T t2 -> R T t3 -> SN (if b t2 t3) /\ (forall s', if b t2 t3 --> s' -> R T s'))) as Hind.
```

Hmm, does `strongly_normalizing_ind` exist with the right shape? Let me check what Coq generates. The inductive:
```
Inductive strongly_normalizing : tm -> Prop :=
  | SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> strongly_normalizing t.
```
The generated induction principle:
```
strongly_normalizing_ind :
  forall P : tm -> Prop,
    (forall t : tm,
        (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') ->
        (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
    forall t : tm, strongly_normalizing t -> P t
```
Yes, because the recursive argument is under a forall, Coq puts the IH as `forall t', step t t' -> P t'` (this is the standard behavior for nested recursive occurrences in a forall — Coq generates the "induction principle with nested IHs" only if it can; for Acc it does exactly this). ```

```
  intros t T H.

Qed.
```

**step_if_inv:**

```
Lemma step_if_inv : forall b t2 t3 s,
  tm_if b t2 t3 --> s ->
  (b = tm_true /\ s = t2) \/ (b = tm_false /\ s = t3) \/ (exists b', b --> b' /\ s = tm_if b' t2 t3).
```

Let's check inversion cases: ST_IfTrue: `tm_if tm_true t1 t2 --> t1` with lc premises; match source: b = tm_true, t2(param)=t1, t3(param)=t2, s = t1 = t2(param). Let me write it.

```coq
(** * Auxiliary definitions for the normalization proof. *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.

Fixpoint subst (x : atom) (u : tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else tm_fvar y
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst x u t1) (subst x u t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst x u t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst x u t1) (subst x u t2) (subst x u t3)
  end.

Fixpoint subst_all (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar y => rho y
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_all rho t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2) (subst_all rho t3)
  end.

Definition update_env (rho : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else rho y.

Definition rho_id : atom -> tm := fun x => tm_fvar x.

Lemma subst_all_id : forall t, subst_all rho_id t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.
```

Hmm, `congruence` might solve the cases. For bvar: subst_all rho_id (bvar n) = bvar n ✓ definitional. For fvar: rho_id x = tm_fvar x ✓. app: IH. abs: IH. Fine, `induction t; simpl; congruence` should work (or `auto`).

Then:

```coq
Lemma in_fold_max : forall (l : list atom) (x : atom), In x l -> x <= fold_right max 0 l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| a l IH]; intros x Hin; simpl in *.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct Hin as [Heq | Hin].
    + subst; lia.
    + apply IH in Hin. lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall (l : list atom), exists x : atom, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  intros l. exists (S (fold_right max 0 l)).
  intros Hin. apply in_fold_max in Hin. lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh_two : forall l1 l2 : list atom, exists x, ~ In x l1 /\ ~ In x l2.
Proof.
  intros l1 l2. destruct (exists_fresh (l1 ++ l2)) as [x Hx].
  exists x. rewrite in_app_iff in Hx. tauto.
Qed.
```

Coq detects parameters syntactically: an argument is a parameter if in every constructor, it's a variable bound outside the constructor's arguments and appears in the same form. That means the induction principle is:
```
lc_at_ind : forall (P : nat -> tm -> Prop),
  (forall k i, i < k -> P k (tm_bvar i)) ->
  (forall k x, P k (tm_fvar x)) ->
  (forall k t1 t2, lc_at k t1 -> P k t1 -> lc_at k t2 -> P k t2 -> P k (tm_app t1 t2)) ->
  (forall k T t1, lc_at (S k) t1 -> P (S k) t1 -> P k (tm_abs T t1)) ->
  ...
  forall k t, lc_at k t -> P k t
```
Note the abs case: P (S k) t1 → P k (tm_abs T t1). So when doing induction with a goal involving a fixed k, the parameter k is fixed. For lc_at_weaken, we want to relate lc_at k and lc_at k' for different k — so we should do induction on the derivation while keeping k' general. Now let's write the R definitions and lemmas.

```coq
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\ (forall u, R T1 u -> R T2 (tm_app t u))
  end.

Definition env_sem (Gamma : context) (rho : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall x, locally_closed (rho x)) /\ (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (rho x)).
```

```coq
Proof. intros T. induction T; intros t HR; simpl in HR; tauto. Qed.

Lemma R_sn : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof. intros T. induction T; intros t HR; simpl in HR; tauto. Qed.
```

The goal `forall t, R T t -> ...` mentions T. `induction T` should generalize the goal over T (the goal is a forall, so no context dependency issue) ✓. Hmm, but `induction T` on a variable in the context with a goal depending on it works ✓.

For T = Ty_Bool: goal `forall t, R Ty_Bool t -> lc0 t`; intros t HR; simpl in HR; tauto ✓ (HR : lc0 t ∧ SN t).

```coq
Lemma R_step : forall T t t', R T t -> step t t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  induction T; intros t t' HR Hstep; simpl in HR.
  - destruct HR as [Hlc Hsn]. split.
    + apply step_lc with (t := t); auto.

  - destruct HR as [Hlc [Hsn Hcl]]. split; [apply step_lc with (t:=t); auto|]. split.
    + destruct Hsn as [t0 Hreds]. apply Hreds. auto.
    + intros u Hu. apply IHT2 with (t := tm_app t u).
      * apply Hcl. auto.
      * apply ST_App1; auto. apply R_lc with (T := T1); auto.
Qed.
```

```
Lemma R_expand : forall T t,
  locally_closed t -> ~ value t -> strongly_normalizing t ->
  (forall t', step t t' -> R T t') -> R T t.
Proof.
  induction T; intros t Hlc Hnv Hsn Hred; simpl.
  - split; auto.
  - split; [auto|]. split; [auto|].
    intros u Hu.
    apply IHT2.
    + (* lc0 (tm_app t u) *) constructor; [auto | apply R_lc with (T:=T1); auto].
    + apply value_app.
    + (* SN (tm_app t u) *) apply SN_intro. intros w Hw.
      destruct (step_app_inv _ _ _ Hw) as [[T0 [body [Heq [Hv Heq']]]] | [t' [Hstep Heq]] | [u' [Hv [Hstep Heq]]]]; subst.
      * exfalso. apply Hnv. rewrite Heq. apply v_abs. auto.
        (* Hmm, need to check the shape *)
      * apply R_sn with (T := T2). apply Hred. auto.
      * exfalso. apply Hnv. auto.
    + intros w Hw.
      destruct (step_app_inv _ _ _ Hw) as [...]; subst.
      * exfalso; apply Hnv; ...
      * apply Hred. auto.
      * exfalso; apply Hnv; auto.
Qed.
```

Let me define step_app_inv carefully:
```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall t u w,
  tm_app t u --> w ->
  (exists T0 body, t = tm_abs T0 body /\ value u /\ w = open body u) \/
  (exists t', t --> t' /\ w = tm_app t' u) \/
  (exists u', value t /\ u --> u' /\ w = tm_app t u').
```
Then in R_expand's cases:
- Left: t = abs T0 body, value u, w = open body u. But Hnv : ~ value t → t = abs T0 body, value (abs T0 body) requires locally_closed (abs T0 body). Do we have it? From Hlc : lc0 t (t is app t u actually... wait no). Hold on: in R_expand, the term is `t` and we're analyzing steps of `tm_app t u`. Hlc : locally_closed t. Lemma subst_all_lc : forall t rho,

```
Lemma subst_all_open_rec : forall rho,
  (forall y, locally_closed (rho y)) ->
  forall t x u k,
    ~ In x (fv t) ->
    subst_all (update_env rho x u) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) =
```

Also, the final proof of `normalization` and how it interacts with the given statement:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T,
  <{ empty |-- t \in T }> ->
  strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T H.
  destruct (fundamental empty t T H) as [Hlc HR].
  apply R_sn with (T := T).
  specialize (HR rho_id).
  apply HR.
  split.
  - intro x. constructor.
  - intros x S Hx. simpl in Hx. discriminate Hx.
Qed.
```

Then `apply HR` leaves goal `env_sem empty rho_id`; split... then after applying, the goal is `R T (subst_all rho_id t)` and I need to rewrite subst_all_id → `R T t`. Better:

```
  assert (Henv : env_sem empty rho_id).
  { split. - intro x. constructor. - intros x S Hx. discriminate Hx. }
  pose proof (HR rho_id Henv) as HR'.
  rewrite subst_all_id in HR'.
  apply R_sn with (T := T). exact HR'.
```

```coq
(* ================================================================ *)
(* Auxiliary definitions and lemmas for the normalization proof.    *)
(* ================================================================ *)

(** Free variables of a term, as a list. *)
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.

(** Single-variable substitution (on free variables). *)
Fixpoint subst (x : atom) (u : tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...

(** Simultaneous substitution. *)
```

Another detail: `induction Hsn as [b Hreds IH]` — the intro pattern names. For SN_intro, the arguments are (t) and the function. For Acc, the induction principle has the form with the IH as an assumption to the constructor case, so `induction H as [x IH]` names x and IH. Right: when the induction principle is

```
Acc_ind : forall P, (forall x, (forall y, R x y -> P y) -> P x) -> forall x, Acc R x -> P x
```
the intro pattern for the case is `[x IH]` where IH : forall y, R x y -> P y. So for strongly_normalizing:

```

  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
```

Hmm, the case argument has 3 things: t, the SN-function, and the P-function. But when Coq generates the induction principle, does it include both the recursive argument (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') AND the IH (forall t', step t t' -> P t')? Yes for nested recursive occurrences under binders, Coq's "induction principles with nested IHs" — hmm, actually, for Acc specifically, Coq generates the standard Acc_ind which I quoted:

```
Acc_ind : forall (A : Type) (R : A -> A -> Prop) (P : A -> Prop),
  (forall x : A, (forall y : A, R x y -> P y) -> P x) ->
  forall x : A, Acc R x -> P x
```
Note: it does NOT include the recursive hypothesis `forall y, R x y -> Acc R y` in the case; it's replaced by the IH. This is because Coq recognizes Acc's structure (the "nested" inductive under a forall is treated specially — the induction principle drops the recursive argument and provides only the IH).

Hmm, actually the general rule: for `Inductive Acc : A -> Prop := Acc_intro : forall x, (forall y, R x y -> Acc y) -> Acc x`, the generated `Acc_ind` is:

```
Acc_ind : forall P : A -> Prop,
  (forall x, (forall y, R x y -> P y) -> P x) -> forall x, Acc x -> P x
```

Yes, that's the standard Acc_ind in Coq's Logic (it's actually defined with a special case). So for `strongly_normalizing`, Coq should generate similarly:

```
strongly_normalizing_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop,
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) -> forall t, strongly_normalizing t -> P t
```

Hmm, but is it really? Coq's generation for a general inductive with a recursive occurrence under a binder: Coq generates the IH as `(forall t', step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t` — i.e., including both, UNLESS the inductive is recognized as an accessibility predicate. Coq has special handling for `Acc`? I recall that for `Acc`, `Acc_ind` in the standard library is defined... In Coq's Logic.v: `Acc` is an inductive, and its induction principle is automatically generated, giving exactly the well-founded induction. The reason it's clean is that Coq's "induction principle with nested recursive arguments" produces:

For `Acc_intro : forall x, (forall y, R x y -> Acc y) -> Acc x`, the generated scheme would be:
```
Acc_ind : forall P, (forall x, (forall y, R x y -> Acc y) -> (forall y, R x y -> P y) -> P x) -> forall x, Acc x -> P x
```
Hmm, which is it? I recall in Coq, `Acc` does have the clean principle (that's why `induction H` for H : Acc R x works without extra hypotheses). Actually no — Coq's `induction H` on Acc DOES work cleanly because `induction` uses `Acc_ind`...

```coq
(* ============================================================ *)
(* Auxiliary definitions for the strong normalization proof.     *)
(* ============================================================ *)

(** Free variables of a term, as a list of atoms. *)
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.

(** Simultaneous substitution of free variables. *)
Fixpoint subst_all (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => rho x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_all rho t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 =>
      tm_if (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2) (subst_all rho t3)
  end.

(** Environment update. *)
Definition update_env (rho : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else rho y.

(** The identity environment. *)
Definition rho_id : atom -> tm := fun x => tm_fvar x.

Lemma subst_all_id : forall t, subst_all rho_id t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.
```

Hmm, `congruence` on the abs case: goal `tm_abs t (subst_all rho_id t0) = tm_abs t t0` with IH `subst_all rho_id t0 = t0` — congruence closes ✓. For if: 3 IHs ✓.

Wait, `induction t` where t : tm and the goal `subst_all rho_id t = t`: the bvar case is `tm_bvar n = tm_bvar n` ✓; fvar: `rho_id x = tm_fvar x` ✓ (simpl reduces rho_id x to tm_fvar x — rho_id is a Definition of a lambda, so beta reduction ✓). OK.

Now the list lemmas:

```coq
Lemma in_fold_max : forall (l : list atom) (x : atom), In x l -> x <= fold_right max 0 l.
Proof.
  induction l as [| a l IH]; intros x Hin; simpl in *.
  - destruct Hin.
  - destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + lia.
    + apply IH in Hin. lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh : forall (l : list atom), exists x : atom, ~ In x l.
Proof.
  intros l. exists (S (fold_right max 0 l)).
  intros Hin. apply in_fold_max in Hin. lia.
Qed.

Lemma exists_fresh_two : forall (l1 l2 : list atom), exists x : atom, ~ In x l1 /\ ~ In x l2.
Proof.
  intros l1 l2. destruct (exists_fresh (l1 ++ l2)) as [x Hx].
  exists x. rewrite in_app_iff in Hx. tauto.
Qed.
```

```coq
Lemma open_rec_lc : forall t k u, lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t).
Proof.
  induction t; intros k u Ht Hu; simpl.
  - (* bvar n *)
    destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst. exact Hu.
    + apply Nat.eqb_neq in E. constructor. inversion Ht. lia.
  - (* fvar x *) constructor.
  - (* app *) inversion Ht; subst. constructor; auto.
  - (* abs *) inversion Ht; subst. apply lc_abs. apply IHt with (k := S k); auto. apply lc_at_weaken with (k := k); auto. lia.
  - (* true *) constructor.
  - (* false *) constructor.
  - (* if *) inversion Ht; subst. constructor; auto.
Qed.
```
Hmm, `apply Nat.eqb_neq in E` — E : (k =? n) = false; Nat.eqb_neq : forall n m, (n =? m) = false <-> n <> m. `apply Nat.eqb_neq in E` gives E : k <> n ✓ (if the lemma's statement is an iff, `apply ... in` should work using the forward direction; I think for iff lemmas, `apply L in H` uses the direction that concludes something matching H's type... H : (k =? n) = false matches the LHS of the iff; `apply Nat.eqb_neq in E` replaces E with k <> n ✓).

Hmm, in some versions it's `Nat.eqb_neq : forall n m, (n =? m) = false -> n <> m` (a one-direction lemma) and `Nat.eqb_eq : forall n m, (n =? m) = true <-> n = m`. Let me check Rocq 9.1's PeanoNat: There are `Nat.eqb_eq`, `Nat.eqb_neq`:
- `Nat.eqb_eq : forall n m : nat, (n =? m) = true <-> n = m`
- `Nat.eqb_neq : forall n m : nat, (n =? m) = false <-> n <> m`
I'm fairly confident these are iff lemmas in modern Coq/Rocq.

```coq
(** STLC call-by-value strong-normalization benchmark task. *)

From Stdlib Require Import Arith.PeanoNat.
From Stdlib Require Import Lists.List.
From Stdlib Require Import Relations.Relation_Definitions.
From Stdlib Require Import Lia.

Module STLCCBVNormalizationTask.

Inductive multi {X : Type} (R : relation X) : relation X :=
  | multi_refl : forall (x : X), multi R x x
  | multi_step : forall (x y z : X),
      R x y ->
      multi R y z ->
      multi R x z.

... etc (original) ...

(** ============================================================ *)
(** Auxiliary development for strong normalization.              *)
(** ============================================================ *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => fv t1 ++ fv t2 ++ fv t3
  end.

Fixpoint subst_all (rho : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => rho x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_all rho t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  | tm_if t1 t2 t3 => tm_if (subst_all rho t1) (subst_all rho t2) (subst_all rho t3)
  end.

Definition update_env (rho : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else rho y.

Definition rho_id : atom -> tm := fun x => tm_fvar x.

Lemma subst_all_id : forall t : tm, subst_all rho_id t = t.
Proof.
  induction t as [n | x | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH1 | | | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 t3 IH3]; simpl; congruence.
Qed.
```

```
Lemma lc_app_inv : forall k t1 t2, lc_at k (tm_app t1 t2) -> lc_at k t1 /\ lc_at k t2.
Proof. intros k t1 t2 H. inversion H. subst. auto. Qed.
Lemma lc_abs_inv : forall k T t, lc_at k (tm_abs T t) -> lc_at (S k) t.
Proof. intros k T t H. inversion H. subst. assumption. Qed.
Lemma lc_if_inv : forall k b t2 t3, lc_at k (tm_if b t2 t3) -> lc_at k b /\ lc_at k t2 /\ lc_at k t3.
Proof. intros k b t2 t3 H. inversion H. subst. auto. Qed.
```

Then step_lc's cases become clean:
- ST_AppAbs: need lc0 (open t v); apply open_rec_lc; [lc_abs_inv applied to lc_app_inv of Hlc | lc_app_inv of Hlc second].
- ST_App1: constructor; [IH (lc_app_inv 1st) | lc_app_inv 2nd].

```
Lemma step_lc : forall (t t' : tm),
    t --> t' -> locally_closed t -> locally_closed t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' H. induction H as
    [ T t0 v Hlcabs Hv
    | t1 t1' t2 Hstep IH Hlc2
    | v1 t2 t2' Hv Hstep IH
    | t1 t2 H1 H2
    | t1 t2 H1 H2
    | t1 t1' t2 t3 Hstep IH H2 H3 ]; intros Hlc.
  - (* ST_AppAbs *)
    destruct (lc_app_inv 0 (tm_abs T t0) v Hlc) as [Hlca Hlcv].
    apply open_rec_lc with (k := 0).
    + apply lc_abs_inv with (k := 0). exact Hlca.
    + exact Hlcv.
  - (* ST_App1 *)
    destruct (lc_app_inv 0 t1 t2 Hlc) as [Hlc1 Hlc2].
    constructor. + apply IH. exact Hlc1. + exact Hlc2.
  - (* ST_App2 *)
    destruct (lc_app_inv 0 v1 t2 Hlc) as [Hlc1 Hlc2].
    constructor. + exact Hlc1. + apply IH. exact Hlc2.
  - (* ST_IfTrue *)
    destruct (lc_if_inv 0 tm_true t1 t2 Hlc) as [Hb [H1 H2]].
    exact H1.
  - (* ST_IfFalse *)
    destruct (lc_if_inv 0 tm_false t1 t2 Hlc) as [Hb [H1 H2]].
    exact H2.
  - (* ST_If *)
    destruct (lc_if_inv 0 t1 t2 t3 Hlc) as [Hb [H2 H3]].
    constructor. + apply IH. exact Hb. + exact H2. + exact H3.
Qed.
```

Wait, careful with the ST_AppAbs case: in the ST_AppAbs constructor, the step's argument `v` is at position 3 with the hypothesis `value v`. In my induction pattern I named them `T t0 v Hlcabs Hv`. But the goal after intros Hlc is `locally_closed (open t0 v)` ✓.

Also the pattern for ST_IfTrue: `| t1 t2 H1 H2` where H1 : locally_closed t1, H2 : locally_closed t2 — and the step is `tm_if tm_true t1 t2 --> t1` ✓.

For ST_If: `t1 t1' t2 t3 Hstep IH H2 H3` where the premises are `t1 --> t1'` (recursive → IH), `locally_closed t2`, `locally_closed t3` ✓.

Hmm, but does the IH pattern order work as `Hstep IH H2 H3`? For the recursive premise `t1 --> t1'`, the induction principle inserts the hypothesis and then its IH. So the order is Hstep, IH, H2, H3 ✓. Same for ST_App1: `t1 t1' t2 Hstep IH Hlc2` ✓.

For ST_App2: premises: `value v1` (non-recursive), `t2 --> t2'` (recursive → hyp + IH). So pattern `v1 t2 t2' Hv Hstep IH` ✓.

```coq
Lemma subst_all_open_rec : forall (rho : atom -> tm),
    (forall x : atom, locally_closed (rho x)) ->
    forall (t : tm) (x : atom) (u : tm) (k : nat),
      ~ In x (fv t) ->
      subst_all (update_env rho x u) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) =
      open_rec k (subst_all rho t) u.
Proof.
  intros rho Hrho t.
  induction t as [n | y | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH1 | | | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 t3 IH3];
    intros x u k Hfresh; simpl.

    rewrite open_rec_bvar, subst_all_bvar.
    destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E; simpl.
    + rewrite subst_all_fvar. unfold update_env. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity.
    + reflexivity.
  - (* fvar *)
    simpl in Hfresh.
    assert (Hxy : x <> y) by (intro Hc; subst; apply Hfresh; simpl; auto).
    rewrite open_rec_fvar, subst_all_fvar, subst_all_fvar.
    unfold update_env.
    destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. exfalso. apply Hxy. exact E.
    + symmetry. apply lc0_open_rec. apply Hrho.
  - (* app *)
    ...
```

Everything is done. Let me write a summary of the proof for the user.

Key points:
- The file Task.v compiles with Rocq 9.1 with no Admitted/Axiom/Abort.
- `Print Assumptions normalization` → "Closed under the global context".
- The proof uses a Tait/Girard logical relation.

Let me summarize the approach concisely:

1. Auxiliary definitions: free variables `fv`, simultaneous substitution `subst_all`, environment update `update_env`, identity environment `rho_id`.
2. Freshness: with atoms = nat, `exists_fresh` / `exists_fresh_two`.
3. Local closure lemmas: weakening, opening preserves local closure, and the inverse "opening with a fresh free variable implies the body is closed at level 1" (`lc_open_inv`), step preserves local closure.
4. The logical relation `R : ty -> tm -> Prop`:
   - Bool: locally closed ∧ strongly normalizing
   - Arrow A→B: locally closed ∧ SN ∧ ∀u, R A u → R B (t u)
   Closed under reduction (`R_step`), neutral expansion (`R_expand`), conditionals (`R_if`), and application of abstractions (`R_abs_app`).
5. Environment semantics `env_sem Gamma rho` (all images locally closed and semantically typed).
6. Substitution lemmas: substitution preserves local closure; commuting opening at a fresh variable with substitution (`subst_all_open_rec`).
7. Fundamental theorem by induction on the typing derivation: every well-typed term is locally closed and semantically typed under every related environment.
8. The target theorem follows by instantiating with the empty context and the identity environment, then projecting SN out of R.

I should mention the one added import (Lia) and that no definitions/statements of the original were changed.

Done.
